========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no... no, I really haven't had any breaks from it. It's just been one long, slow slide, if I'm being perfectly honest with you. It really started... well, like I was saying before, it's been building up ever since I retired from the school office. That was a little over two years ago now. I remember it so clearly because they had that sheet cake for me in the teachers' lounge, the one with the buttercream frosting that was just a little too sweet, and they gave me a very nice engraved clock. 

I thought, well, now I'll have all the time in the world to work on my quilts and tend to my garden. But instead, it just... it started then, this feeling. First it was just taking an extra hour in the morning to get dressed, and then it was deciding maybe I didn't need to dust the parlor every week, and then... well, it just kept growing. It's like a fog that rolled in right after that retirement party and it just never rolled back out. There hasn't been a single month, let alone two, where I woke up and felt like my old self again, ready to tackle the day. It's just been this constant, heavy sitting. Day after day, week after week, for the whole two years since I packed up my desk.

Well, doctor, it's... I suppose it's just that I don't have the push I used to have anymore. I used to be so busy, you know? Always doing something. Well, let me think on that... it's been a while now. I'd say it really started creeping up on me, just building and building, since I retired. I used to work down at the school office, you see. Oh, I was there for years and years. Not teaching, mind you, I didn't have the schooling for all that, but I helped out with the papers and making sure the little ones got to the nurse if they scraped a knee on the playground or had a tummy ache. I just loved seeing those kids every single day, it gave me a real purpose, getting up early and putting my shoes on and getting out the door. 

But then, it was a little over two years ago, I finally retired from the school. And you know, at first, I thought, well, this is going to be real nice, I can just sit and rest my feet for a bit. Because those school floors, they are just solid tile, and they are so hard on your legs when you get to be my age, standing and walking down those long hallways all day. But then a week went by, and a month, and... well, it's been a little over two years now since I left that office. And instead of just resting my feet, it's like my whole body just... stopped wanting to go. 

First I wasn't at the school, of course, and then the choir just felt like too much trouble getting dressed up for, and then the quilting... it just all sort of slipped away, piece by piece, over these last two years. I just sit in that chair thinking about how busy I used to be, with the school bells ringing and the children running in the halls, and now it's just... just the TV humming. So yes, a little over two years it's been building up, ever since I left the school office.

Well... back to my usual self. Let me think on that. I don't... no, I really don't think so. I mean, sometimes I'll wake up and the sun will be shining right through the blinds, and I'll think to myself, 'Today is the day. Today I'm going to get up and go down to the craft store and buy some of that nice yarn, the soft blue kind I used for my nephew's baby blanket.' And maybe I'll even get as far as putting my good slippers on instead of my house shoes. But then... the feeling just washes right out of me, like water down a drain. 

It's just not like it used to be. You know, my sister Sarah came down to visit last Easter, and she brought this big ham with her, a real nice honey-baked one, and she said, 'Come on, we're going to have a proper dinner just like Mama used to make, and we'll get you out of this rut.' And for a minute, just a little minute, smelling that ham warming up in the oven, I thought maybe I was feeling a bit like my old self again. I even got the good plates out of the china cabinet, the ones with the little pink roses around the edges that I only ever use for company. I wiped all the dust off them and set the table. But by the time we actually sat down to eat, I was just so heavy in my bones, and I couldn't even finish my plate. I couldn't keep the conversation going with her. I just wanted to go back to my chair and turn the TV on. 

So, no, doctor... not for a couple of months. Not for a whole week, even. The seasons just keep changing outside the front window, you know? The snow melts and the daffodils pop up by the mailbox, and then it gets hot, and I just watch it all happen from the living room. It's just been this exact same slow, empty feeling, day in and day out, ever since I packed up my desk at the school. I haven't had a real stretch of feeling normal at all.

I just can't seem to bring myself to get out the door anymore.\n\nDoctor: About how long have you been feeling this way and finding it hard to get out of your chair?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think on that... it's been a while now. I'd say it really started creeping up on me, just building and building, since I retired. I used to work down at the school office, you see. Oh, I was there for years and years. Not teaching, mind you, I didn't have the schooling for all that, but I helped out with the papers and making sure the little ones got to the nurse if they scraped a knee on the playground or had a tummy ache. I just loved seeing those kids every single day, it gave me a real purpose, getting up early and putting my shoes on and getting out the door. \n\nBut then, it was a little over two years ago, I finally retired from the school. And you know, at first, I thought, well, this is going to be real nice, I can just sit and rest my feet for a bit. Because those school floors, they are just solid tile, and they are so hard on your legs when you get to be my age, standing and walking down those long hallways all day. But then a week went by, and a month, and... well, it's been a little over two years now since I left that office. And instead of just resting my feet, it's like my whole body just... stopped wanting to go. \n\nFirst I wasn't at the school, of course, and then the choir just felt like too much trouble getting dressed up for, and then the quilting... it just all sort of slipped away, piece by piece, over these last two years. I just sit in that chair thinking about how busy I used to be, with the school bells ringing and the children running in the halls, and now it's just... just the TV humming. So yes, a little over two years it's been building up, ever since I left the school office.\n\nDoctor: During these past two years, have there been any times where you felt back to your usual self for a couple of months or more?\n\nPatient: Well... back to my usual self. Let me think on that. I don't... no, I really don't think so. I mean, sometimes I'll wake up and the sun will be shining right through the blinds, and I'll think to myself, 'Today is the day. Today I'm going to get up and go down to the craft store and buy some of that nice yarn, the soft blue kind I used for my nephew's baby blanket.' And maybe I'll even get as far as putting my good slippers on instead of my house shoes. But then... the feeling just washes right out of me, like water down a drain. \n\nIt's just not like it used to be. You know, my sister Sarah came down to visit last Easter, and she brought this big ham with her, a real nice honey-baked one, and she said, 'Come on, we're going to have a proper dinner just like Mama used to make, and we'll get you out of this rut.' And for a minute, just a little minute, smelling that ham warming up in the oven, I thought maybe I was feeling a bit like my old self again. I even got the good plates out of the china cabinet, the ones with the little pink roses around the edges that I only ever use for company. I wiped all the dust off them and set the table. But by the time we actually sat down to eat, I was just so heavy in my bones, and I couldn't even finish my plate. I couldn't keep the conversation going with her. I just wanted to go back to my chair and turn the TV on. \n\nSo, no, doctor... not for a couple of months. Not for a whole week, even. The seasons just keep changing outside the front window, you know? The snow melts and the daffodils pop up by the mailbox, and then it gets hot, and I just watch it all happen from the living room. It's just been this exact same slow, empty feeling, day in and day out, ever since I packed up my desk at the school. I haven't had a real stretch of feeling normal at all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Well... no. No, doctor, I really haven't. Never in my whole life, I don't think. You know, back when I was a girl... well, I had to leave school early, you see, right in the middle of the eighth grade, because my mama needed help with the little ones at home. There were six of us, and I was the oldest girl, so I just didn't have the time to sit in a chair and feel down, oh my, no. I was up before the sun even thought about rising, making biscuits from scratch, washing clothes... we didn't have those nice machines back then, just a tub and a washboard out back, and let me tell you, that takes the energy right out of your arms, but I always had more to spare. 

And then when I grew up and got my own place, I was always doing something. I kept the house spotless, and I grew this big vegetable garden out back. Tomatoes as big as your two fists, doctor, and cucumbers, and green beans... I'd be canning all summer long in that hot kitchen. The heat would just roll right off your face, but I loved it. I had jars and jars of preserves lined up down in the cellar. I always had energy. Always. Even when I was dog-tired, it was a good tired, you know? The kind of tired where you lay your head on the pillow and you sleep sound because you did a good day's work. Not this heavy, empty tired I have now. This sitting and staring tired. 

So, no, I never had a spell like this before I retired. Not once. It's just so strange to me, because I used to be the one telling everyone else to hurry up and get moving, and now... well, I just don't know where all that push went. It's just entirely new to me, feeling this way.

I keep the TV on, but just real low, barely a whisper, just to have a little noise in the room, I guess. And the thing is... I know people are expecting me. I know the choir director is wondering where I am, and the ladies are waiting on my quilt squares, and I know I ought to be out there visiting... but I just sit there with the TV low. I just can't seem to bring myself to get out the door anymore.\n\nDoctor: About how long have you been feeling this way and finding it hard to get out of your chair?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think on that... it's been a while now. I'd say it really started creeping up on me, just building and building, since I retired. I used to work down at the school office, you see. Oh, I was there for years and years. Not teaching, mind you, I didn't have the schooling for all that, but I helped out with the papers and making sure the little ones got to the nurse if they scraped a knee on the playground or had a tummy ache. I just loved seeing those kids every single day, it gave me a real purpose, getting up early and putting my shoes on and getting out the door. \n\nBut then, it was a little over two years ago, I finally retired from the school. And you know, at first, I thought, well, this is going to be real nice, I can just sit and rest my feet for a bit. Because those school floors, they are just solid tile, and they are so hard on your legs when you get to be my age, standing and walking down those long hallways all day. But then a week went by, and a month, and... well, it's been a little over two years now since I left that office. And instead of just resting my feet, it's like my whole body just... stopped wanting to go. \n\nFirst I wasn't at the school, of course, and then the choir just felt like too much trouble getting dressed up for, and then the quilting... it just all sort of slipped away, piece by piece, over these last two years. I just sit in that chair thinking about how busy I used to be, with the school bells ringing and the children running in the halls, and now it's just... just the TV humming. So yes, a little over two years it's been building up, ever since I left the school office.\n\nDoctor: During these past two years, have there been any times where you felt back to your usual self for a couple of months or more?\n\nPatient: Well... back to my usual self. Let me think on that. I don't... no, I really don't think so. I mean, sometimes I'll wake up and the sun will be shining right through the blinds, and I'll think to myself, 'Today is the day. Today I'm going to get up and go down to the craft store and buy some of that nice yarn, the soft blue kind I used for my nephew's baby blanket.' And maybe I'll even get as far as putting my good slippers on instead of my house shoes. But then... the feeling just washes right out of me, like water down a drain. \n\nIt's just not like it used to be. You know, my sister Sarah came down to visit last Easter, and she brought this big ham with her, a real nice honey-baked one, and she said, 'Come on, we're going to have a proper dinner just like Mama used to make, and we'll get you out of this rut.' And for a minute, just a little minute, smelling that ham warming up in the oven, I thought maybe I was feeling a bit like my old self again. I even got the good plates out of the china cabinet, the ones with the little pink roses around the edges that I only ever use for company. I wiped all the dust off them and set the table. But by the time we actually sat down to eat, I was just so heavy in my bones, and I couldn't even finish my plate. I couldn't keep the conversation going with her. I just wanted to go back to my chair and turn the TV on. \n\nSo, no, doctor... not for a couple of months. Not for a whole week, even. The seasons just keep changing outside the front window, you know? The snow melts and the daffodils pop up by the mailbox, and then it gets hot, and I just watch it all happen from the living room. It's just been this exact same slow, empty feeling, day in and day out, ever since I packed up my desk at the school. I haven't had a real stretch of feeling normal at all.\n\nDoctor: Before these past two years, had you ever experienced a period in your life where you felt down or lost your energy like this?\n\nPatient: Well... no. No, doctor, I really haven't. Never in my whole life, I don't think. You know, back when I was a girl... well, I had to leave school early, you see, right in the middle of the eighth grade, because my mama needed help with the little ones at home. There were six of us, and I was the oldest girl, so I just didn't have the time to sit in a chair and feel down, oh my, no. I was up before the sun even thought about rising, making biscuits from scratch, washing clothes... we didn't have those nice machines back then, just a tub and a washboard out back, and let me tell you, that takes the energy right out of your arms, but I always had more to spare. \n\nAnd then when I grew up and got my own place, I was always doing something. I kept the house spotless, and I grew this big vegetable garden out back. Tomatoes as big as your two fists, doctor, and cucumbers, and green beans... I'd be canning all summer long in that hot kitchen. The heat would just roll right off your face, but I loved it. I had jars and jars of preserves lined up down in the cellar. I always had energy. Always. Even when I was dog-tired, it was a good tired, you know? The kind of tired where you lay your head on the pillow and you sleep sound because you did a good day's work. Not this heavy, empty tired I have now. This sitting and staring tired. \n\nSo, no, I never had a spell like this before I retired. Not once. It's just so strange to me, because I used to be the one telling everyone else to hurry up and get moving, and now... well, I just don't know where all that push went. It's just entirely new to me, feeling this way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, if you mean... if you're asking if I ever think about, you know, not being here anymore? Or wanting to... to end things? Oh, heavens no. No, doctor, I could never, ever do something like that. I know I sit in that chair all day, and I know I just watch the television hum and don't have the gumption to even put my good slippers on half the time, but... well, life is still life, isn't it? 

My mama, Lord rest her soul, she always used to say that every day the good Lord gives you is a day you're meant to see through to the sunset, even if you just spend it looking out the front window. And I think about my sister Sarah, the one who brought that nice honey-baked ham I was telling you about? If I ever did something to myself, oh, it would just break her heart into a million little pieces. Plus, she's got a bad knee, you see—she needs a replacement but she's just terrified of the surgery, she heard some story on the news about it going wrong for a lady her age—so she can't be traveling all the way down here to arrange a funeral and sort through all my things. I have a whole attic full of boxes, doctor. Old quilts I started and never finished, and all those canning jars I mentioned earlier... someone would have to clean all that out, and I just couldn't put that kind of burden on Sarah. 

So no, even though this heavy feeling is just awful, and it feels like I'm wearing a lead coat most days, I don't want to die. I just want to feel like me again. I just want to want to do things, you know? I want to want to go to the craft store. But I'd never try to end it. I just sit there and wait for the day to be done.

I know the choir director is wondering where I am, and the ladies are waiting on my quilt squares, and I know I ought to be out there visiting... but I just sit there with the TV low. I just can't seem to bring myself to get out the door anymore.\n\nDoctor: About how long have you been feeling this way and finding it hard to get out of your chair?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think on that... it's been a while now. I'd say it really started creeping up on me, just building and building, since I retired. I used to work down at the school office, you see. Oh, I was there for years and years. Not teaching, mind you, I didn't have the schooling for all that, but I helped out with the papers and making sure the little ones got to the nurse if they scraped a knee on the playground or had a tummy ache. I just loved seeing those kids every single day, it gave me a real purpose, getting up early and putting my shoes on and getting out the door. \n\nBut then, it was a little over two years ago, I finally retired from the school. And you know, at first, I thought, well, this is going to be real nice, I can just sit and rest my feet for a bit. Because those school floors, they are just solid tile, and they are so hard on your legs when you get to be my age, standing and walking down those long hallways all day. But then a week went by, and a month, and... well, it's been a little over two years now since I left that office. And instead of just resting my feet, it's like my whole body just... stopped wanting to go. \n\nFirst I wasn't at the school, of course, and then the choir just felt like too much trouble getting dressed up for, and then the quilting... it just all sort of slipped away, piece by piece, over these last two years. I just sit in that chair thinking about how busy I used to be, with the school bells ringing and the children running in the halls, and now it's just... just the TV humming. Let me think on that. I don't... no, I really don't think so. I mean, sometimes I'll wake up and the sun will be shining right through the blinds, and I'll think to myself, 'Today is the day. Today I'm going to get up and go down to the craft store and buy some of that nice yarn, the soft blue kind I used for my nephew's baby blanket.' And maybe I'll even get as far as putting my good slippers on instead of my house shoes. But then... the feeling just washes right out of me, like water down a drain. \n\nIt's just not like it used to be. You know, my sister Sarah came down to visit last Easter, and she brought this big ham with her, a real nice honey-baked one, and she said, 'Come on, we're going to have a proper dinner just like Mama used to make, and we'll get you out of this rut.' And for a minute, just a little minute, smelling that ham warming up in the oven, I thought maybe I was feeling a bit like my old self again. I even got the good plates out of the china cabinet, the ones with the little pink roses around the edges that I only ever use for company. I wiped all the dust off them and set the table. But by the time we actually sat down to eat, I was just so heavy in my bones, and I couldn't even finish my plate. I couldn't keep the conversation going with her. I just wanted to go back to my chair and turn the TV on. \n\nSo, no, doctor... not for a couple of months. Not for a whole week, even. The seasons just keep changing outside the front window, you know? The snow melts and the daffodils pop up by the mailbox, and then it gets hot, and I just watch it all happen from the living room. It's just been this exact same slow, empty feeling, day in and day out, ever since I packed up my desk at the school. I haven't had a real stretch of feeling normal at all.\n\nDoctor: Before these past two years, had you ever experienced a period in your life where you felt down or lost your energy like this?\n\nPatient: Well... no. No, doctor, I really haven't. Never in my whole life, I don't think. You know, back when I was a girl... well, I had to leave school early, you see, right in the middle of the eighth grade, because my mama needed help with the little ones at home. There were six of us, and I was the oldest girl, so I just didn't have the time to sit in a chair and feel down, oh my, no. I was up before the sun even thought about rising, making biscuits from scratch, washing clothes... we didn't have those nice machines back then, just a tub and a washboard out back, and let me tell you, that takes the energy right out of your arms, but I always had more to spare. \n\nAnd then when I grew up and got my own place, I was always doing something. I kept the house spotless, and I grew this big vegetable garden out back. Tomatoes as big as your two fists, doctor, and cucumbers, and green beans... I'd be canning all summer long in that hot kitchen. The heat would just roll right off your face, but I loved it. I had jars and jars of preserves lined up down in the cellar. I always had energy. Always. Even when I was dog-tired, it was a good tired, you know? The kind of tired where you lay your head on the pillow and you sleep sound because you did a good day's work. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well... my sleep. It's funny you ask that, because Sarah was just asking me the same thing on the phone last Tuesday. Or was it Wednesday? No, Tuesday, because Wednesday is when the garbage truck comes clattering down the street and wakes me up if I am sleeping. But to answer your question, doctor, my sleep has been... well, it's just fine, really. I don't have any trouble with it. 

I know some folks my age, they complain about tossing and turning, or waking up at three in the morning and not being able to get back to sleep. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she says she has to take one of those little blue pills every single night just to get a wink, and even then she says she wakes up feeling fuzzy in the head. But not me. When I finally get up from my chair in the living room and turn off the television—usually after the late local news, the one with that nice weatherman who always wears the bright ties—I just go into the bedroom, put on my flannel nightgown, and get under the covers. And I sleep just fine. 

I suppose I might sleep a little too much, if you want to know the truth of it. Sometimes I don't get out of bed until ten or eleven in the morning. The sun will be high up in the sky, and I'll just lie there looking at the dust motes floating in the light coming through the curtains. Those curtains, I made them myself, you know, back when I still had the energy to use my sewing machine. They're a lovely pale yellow, meant to look cheerful. But anyway, I just lie there, and I know I should get up, but the bed is warm and that heavy feeling is just sitting right on my chest, so I just stay put. But the sleeping part itself? No, no problems there. I sleep through the night, mostly. Just regular sleep, I suppose. Nothing to complain about on that front.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well... my appetite. You know, it's funny, with everything else feeling so heavy and slow, you'd think I'd stop eating, but... no, my appetite is just about the same as it always was. I haven't noticed any difference at all in how hungry I get. 

I still eat my three meals, regular as a clock. Now, I don't cook those big, heavy meals like I used to, of course. Oh my, no. I used to make a whole pot roast on Sundays, with the carrots and the little red potatoes roasted right in the pan juices, and a big old bowl of green beans cooked down with a ham hock... the house would smell so good all afternoon, you could smell it clear out to the mailbox. But these days, I just don't have the push to stand at the stove that long. 

But I still eat! I have my oatmeal in the morning, usually around eleven when I finally get out of that bed. Sometimes I put a little brown sugar and milk on it, if I have the energy to reach up and get the sugar bowl down from the top shelf. And for lunch, maybe just a sandwich. Sarah, my sister, she brings over those cold cuts from the deli at the grocery store... she knows I like the honey turkey, not the smoked kind, because the smoked kind always gives me a terrible bit of heartburn if I eat it too late in the day. So I just make a little sandwich and eat it on a tray right there in my lap while I sit in my chair and watch the television. And for supper, maybe I'll just heat up a can of chicken noodle soup or have a slice of that ham Sarah brought. 

So I'm getting my food in, same as always. I'm not wasting away or anything like that, and I'm not overeating either. My clothes still fit exactly the same as they did before all this started. I bought the very slacks I'm wearing right now at JCPenney back in... oh, it must have been five or six years ago, they had a wonderful sale on the clearance rack, and they still button up just fine, no tighter and no looser. So no, doctor, no changes with my appetite. I just don't have the joy in making the food anymore, but the eating part hasn't changed a bit.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF34.1, F32\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it's... I suppose it's just that I don't have the push I used to have anymore. I used to be so busy, you know? Always doing something. I'd be down at the church for choir, singing with everybody, and then I had my quilting... we used to make these big, beautiful quilts, me and the ladies. And I was always the first one to go visiting folks if they were feeling poorly or just needed some company. I loved doing all that, it was my whole week. \n\nBut lately... I just, well, I can't seem to find the energy for any of it. The push is just gone. Most days, from the time I wake up till the sun goes down, I just sit at home in my chair. I keep the TV on, but just real low, barely a whisper, just to have a little noise in the room, I guess. And the thing is... I know people are expecting me. I know the choir director is wondering where I am, and the ladies are waiting on my quilt squares, and I know I ought to be out there visiting... but I just sit there with the TV low. I just can't seem to bring myself to get out the door anymore.\n\nDoctor: About how long have you been feeling this way and finding it hard to get out of your chair?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think on that... it's been a while now. I'd say it really started creeping up on me, just building and building, since I retired. I used to work down at the school office, you see. Oh, I was there for years and years. Not teaching, mind you, I didn't have the schooling for all that, but I helped out with the papers and making sure the little ones got to the nurse if they scraped a knee on the playground or had a tummy ache. I just loved seeing those kids every single day, it gave me a real purpose, getting up early and putting my shoes on and getting out the door. \n\nBut then, it was a little over two years ago, I finally retired from the school. And you know, at first, I thought, well, this is going to be real nice, I can just sit and rest my feet for a bit. Because those school floors, they are just solid tile, and they are so hard on your legs when you get to be my age, standing and walking down those long hallways all day. But then a week went by, and a month, and... well, it's been a little over two years now since I left that office. And instead of just resting my feet, it's like my whole body just... stopped wanting to go. \n\nFirst I wasn't at the school, of course, and then the choir just felt like too much trouble getting dressed up for, and then the quilting... it just all sort of slipped away, piece by piece, over these last two years. I just sit in that chair thinking about how busy I used to be, with the school bells ringing and the children running in the halls, and now it's just... just the TV humming. So yes, a little over two years it's been building up, ever since I left the school office.\n\nDoctor: During these past two years, have there been any times where you felt back to your usual self for a couple of months or more?\n\nPatient: Well... back to my usual self. Let me think on that. I don't... no, I really don't think so. I mean, sometimes I'll wake up and the sun will be shining right through the blinds, and I'll think to myself, 'Today is the day. Today I'm going to get up and go down to the craft store and buy some of that nice yarn, the soft blue kind I used for my nephew's baby blanket.' And maybe I'll even get as far as putting my good slippers on instead of my house shoes. But then... the feeling just washes right out of me, like water down a drain. \n\nIt's just not like it used to be. You know, my sister Sarah came down to visit last Easter, and she brought this big ham with her, a real nice honey-baked one, and she said, 'Come on, we're going to have a proper dinner just like Mama used to make, and we'll get you out of this rut.' And for a minute, just a little minute, smelling that ham warming up in the oven, I thought maybe I was feeling a bit like my old self again. I even got the good plates out of the china cabinet, the ones with the little pink roses around the edges that I only ever use for company. I wiped all the dust off them and set the table. But by the time we actually sat down to eat, I was just so heavy in my bones, and I couldn't even finish my plate. I couldn't keep the conversation going with her. I just wanted to go back to my chair and turn the TV on. \n\nSo, no, doctor... not for a couple of months. Not for a whole week, even. The seasons just keep changing outside the front window, you know? The snow melts and the daffodils pop up by the mailbox, and then it gets hot, and I just watch it all happen from the living room. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well... about myself as a person? I suppose I feel... just fine about who I am. I mean, I know I'm not exactly setting the world on fire these days, just sitting in my chair and watching my programs, but I don't sit there thinking bad thoughts about myself, if that's what you're getting at. 

My mama always said, "You are exactly who the good Lord made you to be," and I've always held on to that. Even when I was a little girl and had to leave school early—I never even made it to the high school, you see, because I had to help out at home when my daddy hurt his back at the lumber mill and things got so tight for us—I never felt less-than. I've always known I was a good person. I've worked hard all my life, I always kept a clean house before this heavy feeling set in, and I never gossip maliciously about the neighbors. Well... maybe just a little bit about Mrs. Gable's front yard, because she just lets those dandelions grow knee-high and they blow right over into my flower beds, but that's neither here nor there. 

What I mean to say is, I don't sit around beating myself up. I don't feel worthless or anything awful like that. Sarah, my sister, she was just telling me on the phone the other day, she said, "You're a good woman, you just lost your spark right now." And I think she's right. I know I'm still me deep down. I'm just a good woman who can't seem to find the gumption to do much of anything besides watch the television. The heaviness doesn't make me hate myself, it just makes me... still. So I feel perfectly fine about me. Just fine.

Today I'm going to get up and go down to the craft store and buy some of that nice yarn, the soft blue kind I used for my nephew's baby blanket.' And maybe I'll even get as far as putting my good slippers on instead of my house shoes. But then... the feeling just washes right out of me, like water down a drain. \n\nIt's just not like it used to be. You know, my sister Sarah came down to visit last Easter, and she brought this big ham with her, a real nice honey-baked one, and she said, 'Come on, we're going to have a proper dinner just like Mama used to make, and we'll get you out of this rut.' And for a minute, just a little minute, smelling that ham warming up in the oven, I thought maybe I was feeling a bit like my old self again. I even got the good plates out of the china cabinet, the ones with the little pink roses around the edges that I only ever use for company. I wiped all the dust off them and set the table. But by the time we actually sat down to eat, I was just so heavy in my bones, and I couldn't even finish my plate. I couldn't keep the conversation going with her. I just wanted to go back to my chair and turn the TV on. \n\nSo, no, doctor... not for a couple of months. Not for a whole week, even. The seasons just keep changing outside the front window, you know? The snow melts and the daffodils pop up by the mailbox, and then it gets hot, and I just watch it all happen from the living room. It's just been this exact same slow, empty feeling, day in and day out, ever since I packed up my desk at the school. I haven't had a real stretch of feeling normal at all.\n\nDoctor: Before these past two years, had you ever experienced a period in your life where you felt down or lost your energy like this?\n\nPatient: Well... no. No, doctor, I really haven't. Never in my whole life, I don't think. You know, back when I was a girl... well, I had to leave school early, you see, right in the middle of the eighth grade, because my mama needed help with the little ones at home. There were six of us, and I was the oldest girl, so I just didn't have the time to sit in a chair and feel down, oh my, no. I was up before the sun even thought about rising, making biscuits from scratch, washing clothes... we didn't have those nice machines back then, just a tub and a washboard out back, and let me tell you, that takes the energy right out of your arms, but I always had more to spare. \n\nAnd then when I grew up and got my own place, I was always doing something. I kept the house spotless, and I grew this big vegetable garden out back. Tomatoes as big as your two fists, doctor, and cucumbers, and green beans... I'd be canning all summer long in that hot kitchen. The heat would just roll right off your face, but I loved it. I had jars and jars of preserves lined up down in the cellar. I always had energy. Always. Even when I was dog-tired, it was a good tired, you know? The kind of tired where you lay your head on the pillow and you sleep sound because you did a good day's work. Not this heavy, empty tired I have now. This sitting and staring tired. \n\nSo, no, I never had a spell like this before I retired. Not once. It's just so strange to me, because I used to be the one telling everyone else to hurry up and get moving, and now... well, I just don't know where all that push went. It's just entirely new to me, feeling this way.\n\nDoctor: ```json\n{\"intent\": \"Assess for suicidal ideation or thoughts of death to distinguish major depressive episode from dysthymia\", \"question\": \"With everything feeling so\n\nPatient: Well, if you mean... if you're asking if I ever think about, you know, not being here anymore? Or wanting to... to end things? Oh, heavens no. No, doctor, I could never, ever do something like that. I know I sit in that chair all day, and I know I just watch the television hum and don't have the gumption to even put my good slippers on half the time, but... well, life is still life, isn't it? \n\nMy mama, Lord rest her soul, she always used to say that every day the good Lord gives you is a day you're meant to see through to the sunset, even if you just spend it looking out the front window. And I think about my sister Sarah, the one who brought that nice honey-baked ham I was telling you about? If I ever did something to myself, oh, it would just break her heart into a million little pieces. Plus, she's got a bad knee, you see—she needs a replacement but she's just terrified of the surgery, she heard some story on the news about it going wrong for a lady her age—so she can't be traveling all the way down here to arrange a funeral and sort through all my things. I have a whole attic full of boxes, doctor. Old quilts I started and never finished, and all those canning jars I mentioned earlier... someone would have to clean all that out, and I just couldn't put that kind of burden on Sarah. \n\nSo no, even though this heavy feeling is just awful, and it feels like I'm wearing a lead coat most days, I don't want to die. I just want to feel like me again. I just want to want to do things, you know? I want to want to go to the craft store. But I'd never try to end it. I just sit there and wait for the day to be done.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been over these past two years?\n\nPatient: Well... my sleep. It's funny you ask that, because Sarah was just asking me the same thing on the phone last Tuesday. Or was it Wednesday? No, Tuesday, because Wednesday is when the garbage truck comes clattering down the street and wakes me up if I am sleeping. But to answer your question, doctor, my sleep has been... well, it's just fine, really. I don't have any trouble with it. \n\nI know some folks my age, they complain about tossing and turning, or waking up at three in the morning and not being able to get back to sleep. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she says she has to take one of those little blue pills every single night just to get a wink, and even then she says she wakes up feeling fuzzy in the head. But not me. When I finally get up from my chair in the living room and turn off the television—usually after the late local news, the one with that nice weatherman who always wears the bright ties—I just go into the bedroom, put on my flannel nightgown, and get under the covers. And I sleep just fine. \n\nI suppose I might sleep a little too much, if you want to know the truth of it. Sometimes I don't get out of bed until ten or eleven in the morning. The sun will be high up in the sky, and I'll just lie there looking at the dust motes floating in the light coming through the curtains. Those curtains, I made them myself, you know, back when I still had the energy to use my sewing machine. They're a lovely pale yellow, meant to look cheerful. But anyway, I just lie there, and I know I should get up, but the bed is warm and that heavy feeling is just sitting right on my chest, so I just stay put. But the sleeping part itself? I haven't noticed any difference at all in how hungry I get. \n\nI still eat my three meals, regular as a clock. Now, I don't cook those big, heavy meals like I used to, of course. Oh my, no. I used to make a whole pot roast on Sundays, with the carrots and the little red potatoes roasted right in the pan juices, and a big old bowl of green beans cooked down with a ham hock... the house would smell so good all afternoon, you could smell it clear out to the mailbox. But these days, I just don't have the push to stand at the stove that long. \n\nBut I still eat! I have my oatmeal in the morning, usually around eleven when I finally get out of that bed. Sometimes I put a little brown sugar and milk on it, if I have the energy to reach up and get the sugar bowl down from the top shelf. And for lunch, maybe just a sandwich. Sarah, my sister, she brings over those cold cuts from the deli at the grocery store... she knows I like the honey turkey, not the smoked kind, because the smoked kind always gives me a terrible bit of heartburn if I eat it too late in the day. So I just make a little sandwich and eat it on a tray right there in my lap while I sit in my chair and watch the television. And for supper, maybe I'll just heat up a can of chicken noodle soup or have a slice of that ham Sarah brought. \n\nSo I'm getting my food in, same as always. I'm not wasting away or anything like that, and I'm not overeating either. My clothes still fit exactly the same as they did before all this started. Even when I was a little girl and had to leave school early—I never even made it to the high school, you see, because I had to help out at home when my daddy hurt his back at the lumber mill and things got so tight for us—I never felt less-than. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well... the future. Hopeless? No, I wouldn't use a word like that. Hopeless is such a big, dark word, isn't it? It sounds like the end of the world, like when they talk about those terrible storms on the news, the ones that just wash whole towns away and leave nothing but splinters. I don't feel like that at all. 

I mean, I don't exactly sit in my chair planning out grand adventures or anything. I'm not looking through travel brochures thinking I'm going to take a cruise to Alaska, though Sarah went on one of those about ten years ago and brought me back a very nice little wooden totem pole that sits right on my television set. She said the glaciers were just magnificent, all blue and shining in the sun. But I don't see myself doing anything like that, and I don't really think about what next year or the year after is going to look like. It's more just... taking it one day at a time, I suppose. 

When I wake up and look at those yellow curtains I made, I just think about getting through that day. Having my oatmeal, watching my programs... I like the game shows in the morning, the ones where people guess the prices of things. It's amazing how much a simple can of soup costs these days, really, it's a crime. But I just watch those, and I don't really think about whether tomorrow will be exactly the same or if this heavy feeling will ever lift. I just don't give it much thought either way. 

So I'm not sitting here wringing my hands, thinking things will never get better, or crying over what's to come. I'm just... here. Doing what I do today. I suppose you could say I don't expect much to change, but it doesn't make me feel hopeless. It's just the way things are right now. Like I said, I'm just sitting still.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well... my concentration. No, I wouldn't say I have any trouble with that. My mind hasn't gone fuzzy on me, if that's what you're asking. 

I can sit and watch my television programs and I don't lose the plot at all. I watch my stories every afternoon right after I finish that little sandwich I told you about. I've been watching the same story for, oh, it must be forty years now. Since back when my husband was still working at the hardware store and I'd have the ironing board set up in the living room. And I follow along just fine. Just the other day, one of the characters, she was trying to hide a terrible secret from her own sister about a business deal gone bad, and I remembered exactly what happened three weeks ago that she was trying to cover up. Sarah, my sister, she sometimes forgets who is married to who on that show, but I always have to remind her. So my mind is still sharp as a tack when it comes to paying attention. 

And as for making decisions... well, I don't exactly have a whole lot of big, world-changing decisions to make these days, just sitting in my chair. But the everyday ones? Oh, I manage those just perfectly. Like this morning, I had to decide whether to put on this blue cardigan or my pink one. I looked out the window and saw those dark clouds rolling in over the Miller's roof next door, and I thought to myself, it's going to be terribly drafty in the house today, so I chose the blue one because it's a much thicker knit. Sarah knitted this for me for my sixtieth birthday, actually. It has these lovely little pearl buttons she found at a craft fair down in the county seat. 

So no, I don't have any trouble deciding things like that, or deciding what soup to heat up. My mind doesn't wander off, and I don't get confused or stuck when I need to pick something. I just do everything a bit slower because of that heavy, still feeling I have. But the thinking part? The focusing part? That's all working exactly the way it always has.

---------- turn 10 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=gemini-3.8-flash ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF34.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it's... I suppose it's just that I don't have the push I used to have anymore. I used to be so busy, you know? Always doing something. I'd be down at the church for choir, singing with everybody, and then I had my quilting... we used to make these big, beautiful quilts, me and the ladies. And I was always the first one to go visiting folks if they were feeling poorly or just needed some company. I loved doing all that, it was my whole week. \n\nBut lately... I just, well, I can't seem to find the energy for any of it. The push is just gone. Most days, from the time I wake up till the sun goes down, I just sit at home in my chair. I keep the TV on, but just real low, barely a whisper, just to have a little noise in the room, I guess. And the thing is... I know people are expecting me. I know the choir director is wondering where I am, and the ladies are waiting on my quilt squares, and I know I ought to be out there visiting... but I just sit there with the TV low. I just can't seem to bring myself to get out the door anymore.\n\nDoctor: About how long have you been feeling this way and finding it hard to get out of your chair?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think on that... it's been a while now. I'd say it really started creeping up on me, just building and building, since I retired. I used to work down at the school office, you see. Oh, I was there for years and years. Not teaching, mind you, I didn't have the schooling for all that, but I helped out with the papers and making sure the little ones got to the nurse if they scraped a knee on the playground or had a tummy ache. I just loved seeing those kids every single day, it gave me a real purpose, getting up early and putting my shoes on and getting out the door. \n\nBut then, it was a little over two years ago, I finally retired from the school. And you know, at first, I thought, well, this is going to be real nice, I can just sit and rest my feet for a bit. Because those school floors, they are just solid tile, and they are so hard on your legs when you get to be my age, standing and walking down those long hallways all day. But then a week went by, and a month, and... well, it's been a little over two years now since I left that office. And instead of just resting my feet, it's like my whole body just... stopped wanting to go. \n\nFirst I wasn't at the school, of course, and then the choir just felt like too much trouble getting dressed up for, and then the quilting... it just all sort of slipped away, piece by piece, over these last two years. I just sit in that chair thinking about how busy I used to be, with the school bells ringing and the children running in the halls, and now it's just... just the TV humming. So yes, a little over two years it's been building up, ever since I left the school office.\n\nDoctor: During these past two years, have there been any times where you felt back to your usual self for a couple of months or more?\n\nPatient: Well... back to my usual self. Let me think on that. I don't... no, I really don't think so. I mean, sometimes I'll wake up and the sun will be shining right through the blinds, and I'll think to myself, 'Today is the day. Today I'm going to get up and go down to the craft store and buy some of that nice yarn, the soft blue kind I used for my nephew's baby blanket.' And maybe I'll even get as far as putting my good slippers on instead of my house shoes. But then... the feeling just washes right out of me, like water down a drain. \n\nIt's just not like it used to be. You know, my sister Sarah came down to visit last Easter, and she brought this big ham with her, a real nice honey-baked one, and she said, 'Come on, we're going to have a proper dinner just like Mama used to make, and we'll get you out of this rut.' And for a minute, just a little minute, smelling that ham warming up in the oven, I thought maybe I was feeling a bit like my old self again. I even got the good plates out of the china cabinet, the ones with the little pink roses around the edges that I only ever use for company. I wiped all the dust off them and set the table. But by the time we actually sat down to eat, I was just so heavy in my bones, and I couldn't even finish my plate. I couldn't keep the conversation going with her. I just wanted to go back to my chair and turn the TV on. \n\nSo, no, doctor... not for a couple of months. Not for a whole week, even. The seasons just keep changing outside the front window, you know? The snow melts and the daffodils pop up by the mailbox, and then it gets hot, and I just watch it all happen from the living room. It's just been this exact same slow, empty feeling, day in and day out, ever since I packed up my desk at the school. I haven't had a real stretch of feeling normal at all.\n\nDoctor: Before these past two years, had you ever experienced a period in your life where you felt down or lost your energy like this?\n\nPatient: Well... no. No, doctor, I really haven't. Never in my whole life, I don't think. You know, back when I was a girl... well, I had to leave school early, you see, right in the middle of the eighth grade, because my mama needed help with the little ones at home. There were six of us, and I was the oldest girl, so I just didn't have the time to sit in a chair and feel down, oh my, no. I was up before the sun even thought about rising, making biscuits from scratch, washing clothes... we didn't have those nice machines back then, just a tub and a washboard out back, and let me tell you, that takes the energy right out of your arms, but I always had more to spare. \n\nAnd then when I grew up and got my own place, I was always doing something. Even when I was a little girl and had to leave school early—I never even made it to the high school, you see, because I had to help out at home when my daddy hurt his back at the lumber mill and things got so tight for us—I never felt less-than. Like I said, I'm just sitting still.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty with your concentration, focusing on things, or making everyday decisions?\n\nPatient: Well... my concentration. No, I wouldn't say I have any trouble with that. My mind hasn't gone fuzzy on me, if that's what you're asking. \n\nI can sit and watch my television programs and I don't lose the plot at all. I watch my stories every afternoon right after I finish that little sandwich I told you about. I've been watching the same story for, oh, it must be forty years now. Since back when my husband was still working at the hardware store and I'd have the ironing board set up in the living room. And I follow along just fine. Just the other day, one of the characters, she was trying to hide a terrible secret from her own sister about a business deal gone bad, and I remembered exactly what happened three weeks ago that she was trying to cover up. Sarah, my sister, she sometimes forgets who is married to who on that show, but I always have to remind her. So my mind is still sharp as a tack when it comes to paying attention. \n\nAnd as for making decisions... well, I don't exactly have a whole lot of big, world-changing decisions to make these days, just sitting in my chair. But the everyday ones? Oh, I manage those just perfectly. Like this morning, I had to decide whether to put on this blue cardigan or my pink one. I looked out the window and saw those dark clouds rolling in over the Miller's roof next door, and I thought to myself, it's going to be terribly drafty in the house today, so I chose the blue one because it's a much thicker knit. Sarah knitted this for me for my sixtieth birthday, actually. It has these lovely little pearl buttons she found at a craft fair down in the county seat. \n\nSo no, I don't have any trouble deciding things like that, or deciding what soup to heat up. My mind doesn't wander off, and I don't get confused or stuck when I need to pick something. I just do everything a bit slower because of that heavy, still feeling I have. But the thinking part? The focusing part? That's all working exactly the way it always has."
